Some of its general features can be traced even in the Nonjurors of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, who looked on the Puritan factions with contempt and horror. It has come back in at least two forms in our own day. On the whole it is rather characteristic of Great Britain, so I shall be brief about it here.

The Theocratist believes, as I do, that the Old Testament is the great key to the meaning of national society. He believes, as I do, that the Lord is King of every nation as He was of the Jewish nation. He thinks, as I do, that a nation is to make wars, administer oaths, and inflict punishments in the name and under the authority of its unseen Ruler. From these premises he draws inferences. They vary with his other feelings, tempers and beliefs, but all seem to rest on the same scriptural authority.

The Covenanter says that the Jew existed to witness against idolatry and was to draw his sword to put it down. The modern nation exists for the same purpose and must use the same means. By its obligation and oath to God it is bound to root out every form of faith that is idolatrous or tends to idolatry.

The Fifth-Monarchy Man does not see the pattern of a theocracy in any existing nation. But one is about to be established, which will fulfil the prophecies about the kingdom cut out of the mountain without hands. He is not clear whether this society is to be universal or national, a Catholic Church or an Ancient Israel. He finds features of both in the Bible and tries to unite them.

The Nonjuror recognises the Church and the nation as distinct. But the nation exists in the person of its king, whose purpose is to support and spread the Church and put down heresy. That was the function of the old Jewish king, and it should be the king's function now. For this he is anointed with oil and his person is sacred; he may not be deposed or set aside for any offence.

From what I said about the Sermon on the Mount, the reader may already understand my main point of difference with all these classes, but it is worth bringing out in relation to each. The Covenanter has the strongest warrant for saying that the Jewish Commonwealth was in all its parts a protest against idolatry, and that the moment it forgot that protest it virtually ceased to exist. I think he is also right that every nation exists on condition of bearing this protest. Sensual worship destroys it. Acknowledging an absolute unseen Being, and rigidly excluding whatever would confuse Him with His handiwork, is implied in the very nature of law and is the only footing on which law can be enforced.

A nation, then, must strive against idolatry. But how? Under a national, legal dispensation, where law comes first and spiritual principles are seen as supports of law, the nation must use against idolatry the same means it uses against other offences. Idolatry and all false worship must be crimes punished by the State. This has obvious inconveniences, but it is unavoidable, since the nation's existence depends on it. Under a spiritual dispensation we escape this necessity. Idolatry moves from the circle of outward crimes to the circle of inward sins. The Lawgiver's sword is felt to be inadequate to reach it, and the sword of the Spirit is known to be more effectual. The Spirit claims idolatry as part of its province and leaves to the legislature only outward crimes, which idolatry, if not rooted out of the heart, must cause. So the Covenanting doctrine, that because the Lord is our national King we must treat offences against Him as offences against the law, applies only to a State existing without a Church. Neither God's honour nor the nation's safety binds us to any particular method of avenging the one or preserving the other. If we believe, as believers in a Church must, that spiritual methods are the most Divine, and, as readers of history must, that they are the most effectual, we have no excuse for following Jewish precedent at the cost of sacrificing principles that the Old Testament implies and the New expounds.

It is evident from these considerations, I think, that the Scotch Covenanter was trying to establish a Jewish and not a Christian nation. That is, he wanted a nation professing Religion, not a nation that recognizes a Church as the ground and vital principle of its own existence.

Of the Millennarians I will say only that the records of their thoughts are well worth studying. Their confusions between what is distinct and what is universal, and between what is spiritual and what is legal, are the same confusions we find scattered through all ecclesiastical history, only concentrated in a small compass and stripped of restraining and correcting influences. And in the writings of Sir Harry Vane, if he is to be counted one of them, we may find very deep principles and remarkable distinctions. They need only the acknowledgment that the Catholic Church had embodied them ages before, to be as practically important as they are profound.

It will seem very strange that I should charge the Nonjuror, usually spoken of in England as the highest of High Churchmen, with the same legal, anti-ecclesiastical spirit we saw in the Covenanter. But the fact seems to be this: he thought of the Church as little more than the Canon Law embodied in a set of persons and institutions. He set this law far above the national law and resisted as Erastian any attempt to bring one under the yoke of the other. But of the Church as a spiritual, sacramental body, constituted not in laws but in a Person, showing the principles and essence of laws and so not to be confined by their formalities, he seems to have had little idea. So he gave little thought to how the Nation could serve the Church except by spreading its opinions and putting down its enemies. Nor did he consider how both might serve the purposes of Him who is absolute Love and absolute Righteousness, toward a spiritual creature living here under visible and earthly conditions. The same habit of mind that must have so obscured the New Testament for him seems not to have helped him much with the Old. Otherwise he must surely have seen that, however unlawful it is for subjects to depose kings, Scripture often says that the King of kings uses His own prerogative in deposing them. And if we live under His government as the Jews did (which is the Nonjurors' own doctrine), we must have a right to ask whether He has ever shown displeasure at a monarch who broke a national covenant by setting him aside and appointing another. For it may be that in refusing allegiance to that other ruler, or denying that he is rightful as well as actual king, we are making light not of the privileges of subjects but of the authority of God.

#### [Section III.—The Separatist.]{.smallcaps}

By this name I mean those who say that Nation and Church ought to be separate bodies, though unlike the Quaker they do not regard national life as an evil thing. Their reasons are these: the State is secular; the Church, if it is a true Church, is anti-secular; to unite a secular body with an anti-secular one is monstrous. The results, they say, are an invasion of the rights of conscience, constant disputes between the two societies, and an impossibility of reformation. These are the doctrines commonly held by the successors of those English Puritans who were themselves such vehement asserters of the religious character of the State.

I need not reply at length, for I have granted their premises as fully as they could wish. A secular and an anti-secular society can live together only as deadly enemies. If the State is secular, the Church must desire its extinction, since she lives to destroy what is secular. What puzzles me is how Christian men can speak so tolerantly of something they label with so purely evil a word. If they do not use *secular* in an evil sense, where is the point of their contrast? How is the Church anti-secular, except by being opposed to something wrong and ungodly? If anti-secular means only *spiritual* as opposed to *legal*, the phrase is a cheat. We deny any contradiction between the legal and the spiritual, and so will they: they say constantly that Law and Gospel are not contrary, though the Gospel can do what the Law, weak through the flesh, cannot. They resist, as we do, the idea that what is evangelical is anti-legal, while asserting, as we do, that it is entirely different from the legal because its power is inward and not merely literal. What I have been maintaining is that a nation, just so far as it is a nation, is anti-secular in one way, just as the Church is in another. Both are God's appointed instruments against the evil, rebellious, disorderly principles that make up the Scriptural idea of "this world." Both are open to invasion by the principle they are meant to resist, and both have been infected by it. The Church became secular when she tried to realise herself as a separate body; the Nation became secular when it tried the same. Each did so by violating the law of its existence and refusing to be what Scripture affirms and history proves it was meant to be.

After what I said under the last head, little needs saying about the charge that this union is unfavourable to the rights of conscience; I have already explained how the notion arose. A nation, if it is to survive, must put down whatever undermines it. It finds that principles lead to acts, and that notions about the Supreme Being lead to evil conduct toward men; so it tries to extirpate those principles. Nations in union with the Church have used the sword for this, and Churchmen may have encouraged them. But the nation did not need to do it, and the Churchman should have known he had better powers for repressing such evil. He should have warned the statesman off his province and told him how impotent his arms are against spiritual enemies. Whether he did or not, the joining of his spiritual power with the national power is the great, the only witness to this truth. Separate it from the State, and the ruler, however liberal his notions, will use his power to keep down opinion. Preaching to him about the sin and folly of it will be in vain. He cannot ignore that opinions divide his subjects, make them rebellious, and lead to open mischief. He does not care about them in themselves, but if he is an honest man he cannot refrain from dealing with them by the means he has when the interests entrusted to him are at stake. You say he should leave them to spiritual influences. What influences? You denied that the spiritual had anything to do with the nation, and said the statesman must treat it as entirely unconnected with him.

Here, as everywhere, I have spoken to what I believe is the real feeling of those who raise the objection. They feel there is a Conscience in man, to be reverenced as God's witness in his creature. No truth do I more want to assert. Without conscience, without an inner ear to receive the outer voice, I do not know what the Law would address by its terrors, or the Gospel by its promises of life, or what a Nation or a Church could mean. But is it not curious that those in our day and country who talk most of the *rights* of conscience in their speeches are least willing, in their more learned discourses, to admit the *fact* of a conscience, and even denounce the idea as inconsistent with human depravity? To this all must come who do not believe that man has a constitution, and that his depravity is shown in refusing to abide in it and choosing to be a separate, self-willed creature. We say his Conscience continually protests against his self-will; that the Nation's office is to restrain that self-will by stern and righteous punishment when it breaks out into acts; and that the Church's office is to bring out, by gracious and loving methods, the true free-will of which self-will is the base counterfeit. Likewise the nation's office is to punish the overt acts of folly springing from private judgment that disturb the commonwealth, and the Church's is to teach men to rise above private judgment to the clear, manly judgment that makes a good citizen. Sad when men are *taught* to indulge self-will and selfish judgments, and these evil qualities are clothed with the awful name of *conscience*. Sad for national order and for spiritual life.

I have considered the complaint that the relations of Church and Nation have always produced conflicts earlier in this chapter. The fact is undoubted: the Nation has tried to usurp the Church's prerogatives, and the Church the Nation's. All history is full of it; it is equally full of disputes between parents and children, brothers and sisters, kings and subjects. If we could get rid of relationships, we would not read of their being violated. But can you get rid of them without unmaking God's world and turning it into a wilderness? Can what speaks to man's inner life stand in no connexion with what speaks to him as an inhabitant of this earth? Has he not both spirit and body? Does their constant variance prove there is no law of fellowship between them?

But, it is said, union with the Nation hinders the Church's reformation when she becomes corrupt. The historical evidence for this is particularly weak. Some great reformations were conducted against the civil power, such as Hildebrand's in the eleventh century and many attempted by the religious orders. Others were carried on with civil rulers: the Lutheran in Saxony, the Zuinglian and Calvinistic in Switzerland, and those in Sweden, Holland and England, where the rulers were patrons and promoters. I see evils in all of them, both those done against the civil power and those done by it. But is the English dissenter prepared to say the first kind were much better in conduct and results than the last? If not, he must abandon his principle that the Church can be purified only by standing wholly aloof from the State. If so, he must defend himself as he can from the charge of Popery. To me it is clear, from experience as well as reason, that the State is an excellent admonisher of the Church about her inward corruptions, because it meets the outward evils that are their fruits; and the Church is an excellent admonisher of the State about its sins, because the spiritual man most clearly sees their effect in destroying the nation's heart. But each does mischief if it tries, by its own maxims, to set the other right.

#### [Section IV.—The Patrician, 1.]{.smallcaps}

::: {.hanging}
Idea of a Golden Age.—Allegorical Interpretations of the Old Testament.—Church Discipline.—Extrusion of Heretics.—Catholic Unity amidst National Peculiarities.
:::

There is another class of objectors to my statement, whose opinions deserve great respect. They do not see the union of the Church with the civil power in different nations as a positive evil. It was God's ordinance, and like everything else He appoints, it is to be submitted to. But they say it is false and dangerous to claim that the Church is better for this state of things, or that its circumstances in modern Europe are better than in the first five or six centuries. The age of the Fathers is the pattern shown on the Mount, the true model of a Catholic Church. It had fellowship in faith and worship, discipline for moral offenders, and separation from wilful heretics. Since then the Eastern Church has split from the Western, and Protestants have divided from Romanists. Heresies have been tolerated and discipline made light of. The idea of national Churches has replaced the idea of a universal Church, and in each nation the Church is treated as part of the civil establishment. This condition, they say, is to be borne with humiliation and patience, not spoken of with triumph.

1\. The feeling that the Church once had a golden age, which we may look back to and pray to see restored, is so deeply rooted in men of every circumstance and opinion that only a very thoughtless Christian could treat it lightly. Those who confine that age to the time before Constantine, or to the lives of the Apostles, cannot fairly complain against those who praise the first six centuries. Even the most limited period cannot be called pure. While the Epistles to the Corinthians and Galatians stay in the Canon, indeed until every epistle is stripped of its sharpest warnings, we must admit, as everyone in practice has, that even then there were spots in the feasts of charity. Every form of corruption and every habit that threatens apostasy could be found in Christ's infant family. Shall we then say the glory of the apostolic period lay in the Church's ministers, though not its members? The epistles to the angels of the seven churches get in the way of that. Some of those churches had lost their first love, and some did not exercise discipline. Some tolerated a heresy, which a fairly well supported tradition traces to one of the seven Deacons. Some had a name to live and were dead, and some were neither hot nor cold. Are we ready, then, to give up the feeling that the apostolic periods differed from others? If not, I think we must adopt some such hypothesis as this, which will perhaps satisfy most who give it leisure: *that in the age of the Apostles there was a more distinct and evident conflict with different forms of evil, both outside and inside the Church, than there ever has been since*. It was a critical period, one might say *the* critical period in the history of mankind. It therefore brought all dark images to light beside the perfect light they opposed. This, it may be, is the true idea of the golden age, and the only one the Church's past offers us. It was not an age of innocence but of conflict. It was not holding itself up as a model to the world. It was bringing out the idea of the Church as a body belonging to no age, the permanent witness against the secular spirit that always makes some period of time, rather than the principles shown in it, the object of its admiration.

Surely the history of the first five or six centuries shows the Church in the same kind of struggle. The battle changed its character after Constantine's accession, but who will say it grew less severe? Who will say the Divine image was less brought out through these strifes in men like Athanasius and Chrysostom than in Ignatius or Justin? The whole history of this period is a book of the wars of the Lord. It is precious because it shows strength made perfect in weakness, working itself out in individual men and in all of society. It shows the shouts of the soldiers of the Cross laying low the highest Babel tower ever raised in this world. It shows that God's scheme was to prevail and nothing could withstand it. But the picture is not one of still, beautiful, pastoral life. It is a picture of great crimes and great virtues, often in the same men, always illustrating each other, and always letting us see what man is with God and without Him. God has shown us this age not in particular specimens of excellence but as a whole, and as a whole it is precious. We lose its blessing, and the idea of the Church it represents, if we leave out any of its darker features. So consider what we do when we wish this age restored in fact. If we ask for the age of St. Paul's preaching to return, we ask for Nero to return. If we ask to be taken back to the glorious time of Athanasius, we ask to live under the tyrant Constantius, with the world almost wholly pagan and the Church almost wholly Arian. If we long to sit at Chrysostom's feet, we long for the infamous corruption of Antioch and Constantinople, for the government of Eutropius, and for the villainies of the palace eunuchs. If we think it a blessing to have lived under Augustine's teaching, we must accept Rome sacked by one set of barbarians, the African Church threatened by another, our learning taken from worn-out rhetoricians, and life amid all the seductions and abominations of Manicheism. These considerations are commonplace, but they are true, and for the honour of the Church and its Head we should dwell on them now and then. They also help remove a fantastic habit of mind quite foreign to the temper of the early ages. They cannot make us despise the Fathers, or adopt the notions about them recently spread in this country with more self-conceit than learning, which could only have caught on through some weakness in the theory they opposed. If we are reasonable, the more we see the mass of evil in society that the Church was sent to break down, the more we shall admire the power that broke it down and be thankful for the instruments He used.

2\. But can we say all this and see nothing more than a permission in the national societies which the Church called into being, and with which in the Western world she became identified? Must we not believe it was a mighty step in the development of the Divine scheme, in establishing the Divine kingdom on earth? The Church had been brought out as one body existing in different places to test its strength against the Roman world, and it prevailed. It made good its principle against terrible odds. What is more reasonable than to believe it was to carry on its work of creation along the very line on which the world's work of destruction had run? The Roman empire had swallowed all nations into itself. Its mighty antagonist was to make them breathe again and show each its proper place and function in God's kingdom and commonwealth. Are we told this savours of those godless and intolerable modern theories of progress? I do not care what bad name it is given, provided it justifies God's ways to man and shows the Church has not existed for nothing. But if the charge means we hold a notion of progress inconsistent with the permanence of God's order and truth, no charge was ever so ill supported. *They* are the innovators and the deniers of the permanence of God's order. *They* would make the Church merely a growth out of past and exhausted states of society, who maintain that national life, so honoured in the old world, has been discarded as worthless in the new. We say the Divine mind's counsels did not change in this way, and that the history of modern Europe proves it. We say the Church's constitution, as shown in the conflicts with the Roman empire, was simply a universal constitution. We rejoice that it was, since this showed such a constitution to exist and made plain the meaning of all the past history that had been leading to its discovery. But we say it was necessarily imperfect. It left unaccounted for all the relations that hold men together as neighbours and distinguish them by race and language, and did not bring them under Church influence. Since the Old Testament especially develops these relations, one aspect of that Testament was necessarily lost to the first age of the Church. I know this language will shock some good people, so I will try to explain myself.

3\. It is commonly said that we must look to the Fathers as interpreters of Scripture because they were close to the Apostles and might have heard their very words. I have never understood this doctrine of proximity. It would prove that Athanasius, Ambrose or Augustine must be far less valuable to us than Hermas or Ignatius, and I do not think the Church in any age has felt that. It seems to me rather that the Fathers' great worth came from God placing them in circumstances that especially enabled them to grasp certain great truths, above all the foundation truths about His Being and the order of the Universal Society. On that view I can believe each had his own special merits. The Alexandrian might see what the Latin could not. One principle would come out in power through him who struggled with Arians, another through him who had fought Manicheism in his own heart and in the world. On the other view I think I would risk making an age into a Church, and exalting some individuals of that age above others who perhaps had a more important work.

Whichever view is adopted, it will be hard to prove the Fathers were better placed than other men to understand the circumstances of the *Jewish* nation. They had no *proximity* to that nation's fathers. The Jews they could talk with were of three kinds. Some had let their national feelings dissolve into wider Catholic sympathies. Some were trying to set their old national distinctions against the Church. Some regarded their whole past as little more than a collection of allegories or the development of a Mosaic philosophy. Nor could the Fathers' circumstances give them any sympathy with the old national life of the Jews. The temple was gone and the city laid waste. These events had been necessary to establish the universal Church, and that Church stood in the midst of a great empire in which no nation "moved the wing, or opened the mouth, or peeped." Would it not be reasonable to expect, in such circumstances, the following? In polemic, they could prove clearly that the Jewish Commonwealth was meant as the seed of a great tree. In experience, they could show that the Jewish saints struggled with the same inner enemies that assailed themselves. In mysticism, they could show an invisible guide and teacher training man to know Him through all past ages. But whatever belonged to the common, daily, human life of the Jews would puzzle them utterly, seem out of place in a Divine book, and so of necessity be turned into cabalistic lore. Would anyone not expect this from the Fathers' position? And suppose the facts fit these expectations exactly. Every Christian today who looks into them is puzzled by subtle spiritualisations of facts which delighted him as a child and, as he grew up, seemed to connect with what is going on in the world around him. The Church even of that age condemned one use of this spiritualising method, yet an extravagant use of it was justified in the practice of the most learned and laborious of the Fathers, and it is hard to find where he crossed the legitimate rule. In that case, which is wiser, more pious and more respectful to these holy men? One option is to say they *could not* take in the literal meaning of the old Scriptures so as to give it any life, and that it was not intended they should. The other is to make out a case for them by renouncing our own advantages and resolutely praising a system of interpretation our consciences and hearts keep rejecting, and then, after all, to give up defending it once it is clearly and consistently worked out.

I will not stop to remark, though it must be obvious, that the Fathers' ideas about marriage, property and every institution belonging first to our earthly condition must have been strongly affected by their views of the Old Testament. They would always have looked for the highest, most transcendental ground on which to defend such ordinances. Since the ordinances must exist, they would readily treat them as types of something higher. But they could not see how to connect the type with the actual fact, or how to avoid concluding that whatever is not directly of heaven belongs in some sense to human depravity. It will be impossible for us too, I believe, if we feel no more than a grumbling acquiescence in God's scheme for educating our race. We must acknowledge that by restoring national life through the Church He was carrying out the law of redemption. He was showing that everything belonging to man and his position here, everything that does not violate that position, is comprehended under it.

4\. But it is affirmed that the early Church recognized the discipline of moral offenders as essential to its existence, and that this has almost been lost sight of since the Church entered into fellowship with the nations. I admit at once that the principle of spiritual discipline is implied in the constitution of the spiritual body. This is a discipline for rectifying habits and principles, far deeper and subtler than any lawgiver can exercise, and like the Church's other principles it was first shown to the world in its first ages. I admit further that, alongside this highest power, there is one implied in the existence of every society, and especially needed in the Church. That is the power of noticing the more outward and flagrant transgressions of its members and deciding how far they exclude the offender from its privileges. I think the early Church exercised this power. It was almost the only witness to the degraded, sensualized slaves of a military despotism that any authority in heaven or earth could punish the offences of the high as well as the low. One example like St. Ambrose's sentence on Theodosius was in practical effect worth all the volumes of the Roman jurists. Yet the history of that transaction and the others of that period proves the Church was quite unable to deal with the accumulated profligacy among those admitted to its fellowship. Councils might decide that certain crimes excluded the offender from the Eucharist for five, six or twenty years, yet we know the blackest crimes were committed by clergymen as well as laymen. And everyone must sometimes ask whether the Church, while claiming such lofty, heavenly powers, was not often forced by her circumstances to degrade them and become too much a mere spiritual police.

I readily allow that restoring fixed, positive national law and formal tribunals for punishing crime has made men less aware of the meaning and need of spiritual discipline. The stricter and more definite this law has become, and the more men have accepted it as a fixed, necessary element of their lives, the less the sense of another kind of government, one reaching feelings and character, has prevailed. There has even been a desire to be rid of it altogether. But none of this convinces me that establishing outward law and forming national societies were not part of God's great scheme for developing more fully the nature and character of Christ's kingdom. Looking at it the plain way, I compare any modern European nation, even Russia, with the state of the Roman and Byzantine empires in the first seven centuries. I should tremble at my own ingratitude if I did not wonder at the change God has wrought. I affirm this change is not merely one of outward, material happiness, but is connected with the very ends for which the Church exists. First, it seems to me a positive good that the Church should not be seen as a police, and that another body should perform that function and leave her to find out her own. Second, I believe every circumstance of later European history has been enabling the Church in each nation to discover, if she will, what her powers are. They are far greater than the statesman's, and indispensable to him. In the first age spiritual discipline tried to be everything. Since then outward law has tried to be everything, and spiritual discipline has been forgotten. I showed in my first part how clearly and awfully it has been demonstrated that law cannot do what it wishes and pretends to do. So I think we are coming to a time when the spiritual side of Christ's kingdom must come forward as it has not yet done, and each nation will demand the Church's education and discipline to preserve its own existence. But the Church can profit by this crisis only if she acknowledges that, by the will of her Author and Lord, she is not meant to have an independent existence or to be extranational. She has no commission or powers that dispense with positive, formal law and outward government. Her highest honour is to be the life-giving energy in every body in whose midst she dwells.

#### [Section IV.—The Patrician, 2.]{.smallcaps}

5\. The objection runs: "In old times the Church was able to cast heretics out of her bosom. Once she is connected with the nation, the nation either punishes them by its own crude methods, or else tolerates them and pays no heed to the Church's sentence." I think the same observations apply here. The Church's character and constitution in the early ages showed themselves in her contests with heretics. One partial theory after another was put forward, and one article of the Creeds after another was set aside or pared away. Often against terrible odds, the Church asserted the integrity of her constitution, the spiritual unity that is the ground of her existence. To prevent divisions she was driven to draw subtle distinctions. She often accepted the charge of word-fighting so that she could keep hold of the realities that the word-fighters were emptying out. She often seemed to puzzle the traveller so that she could preserve his inheritance for him. For this struggle she needed her individual champions and her general councils. I have no doubt this was the early Church's work, and that, despite the sins of even her best and bravest members, she fulfilled it as it was meant to be fulfilled.

But does not everyone feel, reading ecclesiastical history, that this position was glorious in some respects and unfortunate in many? In these struggles against those who would divide her, the Church was maintaining her character as a kingdom, held together by its relation to an awful name that she was bound to assert and defend. Yet the strife she entered for this purpose seriously weakened the impression that she was such a kingdom. It led men more and more to imagine that the battle was over a set of dogmas or opinions, which one party of doctors, called Catholics, had agreed to maintain against the world but could not settle among themselves. If Pagans or Jews had taken up this notion, it would have been regrettable. When Christians and their teachers fell into it themselves, the very existence of the Church was endangered. And that is what happened. The Greeks especially, proud of the gift of subtlety that has always been theirs, seem to have lost without noticing the whole substance, though they could distinguish every part of it so accurately. Their zeal and decrees against old heresies called up new ones. They felt less and less that the Church was anything but a school, even while they were using, without scruple, measures that could be justified to their consciences only by the belief that she was the one human fellowship. I think we all feel relief at escaping from this Greek world of controversy into the Latin world of business and enterprise. There may be much evil there too, but practical work is going on, societies are growing, and the Church is felt to be a society governed by certain laws and informed by a certain principle. Who can fail to see that, amid all the contradictions of the Middle Ages, the sense of a common bond, of one spirit and one object, was far more fully realised than it had been, except by a very few of the highest minds, in the age of the Œcumenical Councils? This is what we should expect if we really believed that the Church was under God's government, and that He was making her meaning and power plain amid the perplexities and distractions of human self-will. However strange it may seem, the connexion of Church polity with national polity was what kept this feeling alive. The Greeks could not see the Church as a kingdom because nothing had taught them what a kingdom was. They lived under a despot with whom they had no sympathy, who ruled through creatures and tools rather than as the centre of a social order.

A fact that seems at first to contradict this, and to give a better reason for the Western Church's character, actually confirms it. The Pope, it will be said, made the Middle Ages conscious of their Church unity, and yet the Pope was the great enemy of national societies. I answer that it was the growth of national life in the West that made it possible for a Bishop to take the position the Pope took. Once national feeling was strongly developed, men could no longer see the Church as a mere learned school. They had to see her as a kingdom, since she was exercising a kingdom's powers, and in no other character could they have paid her homage. So the Bishop of Rome could give this kingdom an outward, formal character. He could put himself at its head and act, amid continual reluctance and displays of independence, as feudal superior of the societies into which Christendom was divided. But note the condition: the Pope himself becomes the sovereign of a state, and so honours the very principle he is setting at naught. I feel the anomaly of this. Still, it explains how truly it was God's will that the Nations should come into being, and how necessary this was for the Church's development, not for her chastisement. Returning to the point before us, this also explains the new position of those who differ from the Church's doctrine or order. In the first age they are pronounced guilty of violating the tradition and creed of the universal society. In the second they are treated as invaders of the order and unity of the particular state where they are found, or of the states generally so far as these feel united in a common object. In the third the state judges that it best consults its own peace by leaving them alone, giving them a settled position, and treating them like its other subjects. I have already said why I believe each state will go on doing this only while it keeps its relation to a spiritual society, and why, the moment it becomes a mere civil body, it will resort to force again to put down opinion. But here I am looking at the question in another light, and I ask whether this is not a most important step in God's plans. First, the spiritual society in her general councils took cognizance of schisms and heresies, but she ran the risk of seeming only the maintainer of a system of opinions, not the pillar and ground of truth. She inadvertently cultivated the very spirit of disputation she wished to check. Next, the particular states showed that they were interested in repressing schisms and heresies, thereby testifying that these are practical matters that really interfere with government and order. But in giving this testimony the states also did the Church a great injury. They put themselves in her place, using crude visible weapons for an invisible, spiritual end.

Lastly, the states have held back, or are inclined to hold back, from these experiments, having found them ineffective. But now they are inclined to regard all spiritual matters as dealings between quarrelling schools and sects, with which the national government has little to do. At most, when the quarrels grow violent, it keeps the peace between them, and otherwise leaves them to destroy one another. Now, I say, if there is a Church in the world, these are circumstances in which she can give evidence of her reality that she could not give in either earlier period. She cannot cut off heretics from her communion, for they have cut themselves off and do not care for it. But she can show that she has a secret power within her that may unite them. She can show that the fine distinctions of the Fathers and councils were meant not to cause separation but to prevent it. They were meant to preserve truth in its fullness until a time when men, having tried their different plans and methods of thought, would begin to desire something that could reconcile them. They would then accept no higher evidence of a Divine mission in any body than that it satisfies this aspiration. Nor can the Church make civil rulers see that she has a power of the same kind as theirs, for when they have fought her with her own weapons they have prevailed and she has been foiled. But she may prove to them that she has another power, wholly distinct from theirs and far higher, to which they must turn or perish in their feebleness.

#### [Section IV.—The Patrician, 3.]{.smallcaps}

6\. I will close this head with some remarks on the claim that one Catholic Church cannot be recognized under the limits of national bodies. The objection runs: "How can a Church be one if it has no visible tokens of unity? Those who dream with the Quakers that the Church is a purely spiritual, unseen body may think ceremonial uniformity worthless. But defenders of National Churches want a strict, formal ecclesiastical organisation. Yet on their view it must differ in every country. There can be no general councils, because everything is subject to the will of particular princes, and even provincial synods and national convocations are viewed jealously by the civil power. What can follow but that the Church loses the features by which its identity is recognized in different lands? It adapts more and more to local habits, until it is prouder of what separates it from other communities than of what it shares with them."

My answer begins from what I have maintained against the Quaker: certain permanent *ordinances* express the character and universality of the Church. She therefore does not depend for her unity on the faith and feeling of her members. She bears a constant witness against the lack of faith or feeling in any or all of them. If this is true, and God means *these* ordinances to denote the universal, spiritual society, we should expect him to show the difference between them and whatever is only accidentally attached to them. Some accidents are unavoidable. Baptism and the Eucharist must be administered in *some* mode, there must be rules about the jurisdiction of Bishops, and if there are forms of prayer there must be set forms. Other accidents are not necessary but have become sacred, in parts of the Church or in all of it, for particular reasons. The general legislation of the early Church certainly tended to join a particular way of treating the ordinances to the ordinance itself. I do not say the early Church wanted this. I believe the proceedings and decrees of councils show a cautious and often subtle wisdom in separating what was merely needed institutional arrangement from what belonged to the thing itself. A higher wisdom was also at work, keeping different parts even of an empire like Rome from holding to one ritual as faithfully as they held to the Creed and the ordinances. Still, the effect of ecumenical government, somewhat for those who lived then and much more for us who only read their history, is to blur this distinction. It keeps us from seeing the Divine sign in its own simplicity, apart even from the most desirable and indispensable arrangements about it.

When the Bishop of Rome tried to perpetuate this ecumenical legislation in his own person, the danger became far greater. The signs of the Church were fearfully confused with the ecclesiastical appointments devised to make them effective, and nothing could so obscure the Divine origin and constitution of the Church. Her eagerness to claim power to legislate in emergencies hid the fact that she rested on any principles, or that God had legislated for her. Yet she could not in practice keep up a strictly universal legislation, because local habits, maxims and precedents interfered, which made the confusion of authorities and obligations worse. Then came the Reformation. It asserted God's law as above the Church's law, and when its maxims were perverted it suggested that men's own wills or notions were above both. Meanwhile the Church within each nation had begun in practice to claim power to decree rites and ceremonies. Properly understood and used, that claim was the strongest witness against equating ordinances with ceremonies. It honored the ordinances as gifts to the universal Church. It treated the ceremonies as needful provisions for the spiritual body to manage. That body is bound to teach men by outward evidence that they belong to one family, and must not let its own theories of what suits this purpose mix with the great principles of God's government. Various causes spoiled this witness. When a national ecclesiastical body was checked by individuals unwilling to submit to the rules every society needs, rules that are especially sacred in a society meant to be the pattern of human fellowship, it pressed its pretensions harder and identified what it decreed with the very being of the Church. This was especially likely in nations with a strict civil organisation, always exercising their powers of legislation. The ecclesiastical body would catch the habit of thinking it existed merely to legislate. It would be prouder of its rules for its own government than of all its influence on the heart of society. If such a mind became common, I can imagine no greater mercy than the civil power stepping in to stop clerical convocations or discourage provincial synods. Without such violence I cannot see how a national Church could have learned what its own powers were. I think it would have been crushed under its own decrees and, above all, lost sight of the only grounds of unity it can have with other nations. On the other hand, this discipline has shown, I think, what the substance and essence of a national Church are. Its substance is given by the ordinances it holds in common with Christians elsewhere. Its essence is the powers it shares with the other parts of the body, which are to be used first for the good of its own country. When this lesson is well learned, I have no doubt that each national Church will recover its synods and convocations, because it will then know how to use them. It will not use them from a lust for legislation, or in hope of reaching its chief aims by decrees. It will use them to satisfy scruples. It will use them to lead men away from restless study of externals, by not forcing them to arrange and debate these for themselves. It will use them to fix the ceremonies that best express the Church's great ideas for a people of a particular climate, character and constitution. It will use them to establish and direct discipline and education more effectively. And it will use them to promote fellowship with national Churches willing to own themselves parts of one Catholic body.

Whether national Churches, once they understand their position, may again send representatives to a general council, and whether the princes may find this helpful instead of harmful to their aims, I will not inquire. I see no reason why we might not reach such a result if we follow God's method. But I see the strongest reason why, if we violate that method and seek an independent existence, no councils, compacts or projects of union can be anything but evil in themselves and full of mischief.

Should we then say that Church unity belonged to the first six centuries, and that since national distinctions began there have been no traces of it? We have been able to see, I think, that the separation of the Greek and Latin Churches, so often lamented and a cause of shame and humiliation to both, nevertheless led to very blessed results. It freed the Church from a cruel and mischievous tyranny and let it develop its powers under freer and happier conditions. Likewise, if we consider the subject calmly and solemnly, with repentance for our sins and thankfulness for our mercies, I believe we shall see that without the sixteenth-century Reformation, European society must have sunk into the state of an infidel world. It would have been nominally ruled by the intriguing head of a little Italian principality, and really divided into warring states, each pursuing the most selfish ends and using religion only as a means to them. Since then it has been clear to thinking people that two principles struggle for supremacy in Christendom. One is embodied in Protestantism. It resists any claim of the spiritual power to rule beyond the nation, and always tends to set spiritual authority aside altogether. The other is embodied in Romanism. It resists the attempts of particular states to cut their subjects off from the rest of Christendom, keeps striving to uphold the Church as a separate power, and sets at naught the existence of each nation. These principles have fought for centuries. Suppose it is God's purpose in our age to reconcile them, and to cast out of each the element contrary to his will, which men's perversity brought in. Shall we then whine about not being born when the Church was making its first struggling efforts to assert its unity? Shall we not rejoice and give thanks that we are born in these latter days, when all things hasten to their consummation, and when the unity of the Church shall be shown to be the ground on which all unity in nations and in the heart of man rests?

#### [Section V.—The Modern Statesman.]{.smallcaps}

Such statements, however unwelcome to many Churchmen, will not win over modern politicians or ease their fear of ecclesiastical influence. They may even say that the power I claim for the spiritual body is more dangerous than the one I give up. 'A statesman has to admit that a dominant hierarchy exists, and has little right to complain, since in ordinary times it helps keep other sects quiet and clergymen indifferent. But when you say this hierarchy is the proper educator of the land, you are not content to humour it with a toy. You want it to become practically and politically mischievous. A power that can educate a land must rule over its families, arts, literature, science, ethics and philosophy, and, as head of the professional classes, in one sense over its law and medicine. No government today can tolerate such claims except where circumstances compel it. Religious bodies must affect a country's education for good or ill, but they are happily broken into fragments, and the statesman's business is to see that each acts only as a religious body. The general education of the land he must gradually take under his own direction, after first removing the old prejudices and institutions in the way. If he lets any ecclesiastical body superintend it, that body becomes as dangerous as the Jesuits ever were. For their real power lay not in governing courts but in the schools, and less in the poison they poured into monarchs' ears than in the maxims of ecclesiastical submission and craft they gave children along with their primers and grammars.'

In the first division of this work I traced the political and philosophical movements that have led men today to see education as the only adequate means of preserving government and society. I showed that the line of thought leading to this opinion contradicts the practical results it seems to promise. The statesman wants education as a power to act on men's spirits, yet the statesman of our day has formally repudiated any claim to deal with men's spirits and has blamed his forefathers for assuming it. So when he claims to be the educator of his land, he is in a strange contradiction, though a natural one. He found that warring religious bodies could not exercise the influence over his subjects that he feels they need. He wants them united and harmonised, and a sect-educator sets them at strife. So he leaves the religious bodies the right to teach their own dogmas and takes under his own cognizance whatever else education includes. This course is awkward and inconvenient, even destructive of the very idea of education. Yet it seemed the only one left to him, unless some spiritual body existed in the heart of his nation, as organic as the civil body and able to do what the civil body confesses it cannot. Our later inquiries have led us to believe that there is such a body: it has established itself in every European nation, has taught every nation, and has incorporated itself with civil society. The statesman finds society everywhere bearing witness to it, and must depose it from its functions before he can become schoolmaster himself. That is where the question stands, and since it has been discussed elsewhere I shall not treat it at large. But I need to indicate the difference between the education given by a national Church that understands its own powers and responsibilities and that given, first, by the State, second, by a set of sects, third, by an extra-national ecclesiastical order like the Jesuits. Following the order of the supposed objections, I will speak briefly of how each affects family life, science, art and literature, popular ethics, and philosophy.

I\. 1\. I have spoken more than once about the first of these. I have shown that a mere religious body, such as the Quakers or Calvinists, may reverence family life and at certain stages may even preserve it in great purity, but cannot connect the institution of the family, as such, with its religion. The religious man is one who chooses for himself, who at a certain time has been led to seek a new life and a new fellowship expressing it. That is how a sect begins. The notion is greatly modified once the sect is established: hereditary feelings develop, and deserting the faith of one's forefathers begins to be called an evil. Still, the society subsists on the same principle, and those admitted to its privileges do not grow into them. The religious body is regarded as different in kind from the family. So every sect complains that where hereditary habit prevails, religion becomes a matter of course and loses its power, and violent efforts are needed to revive it. I maintain that such influences cannot cultivate a nation's family life, because they do not bring the spiritual life into direct relation with natural kinsmanship. Many influences now threaten household sanctities, and many schemes of universal society cast them aside altogether. A statesman who has no better protection for them than the teaching of sects must expect to see them perish. Where there are no political motives, no trade-temper, no grand philosophical generalisations, religious men in the sects may hope to keep respect and attachment alive in their children. Where these are abroad, they must tremble, and so must anyone who believes the nation's existence depends on the domestic relations if sects are their only guardians.

2\. But what can the statesman himself do by his education to protect these relations? Nothing. Must he not rather do much to shake confidence in them and impair their sacredness? It is no fault of his; it is the necessity of his position that he aims at making men citizens. He cannot teach them to be sons and brothers, and he has to interfere with the duties that belong to those roles. He must found his schools on the express principle that parents are not competent to teach or to choose teachers, and he must treat the father's authority as if its sacredness depended on the authority of the law. All wise statesmen of antiquity felt this difficulty and were glad of any means of escaping it. Modern statesmen should ask themselves earnestly whether any such help is within their reach.

3\. Most of those I am arguing with will acknowledge that the Jesuit is not the person to whom family life can safely be entrusted. They may also agree, and may wonder that I concede it, that the evil of his influence comes not solely, or perhaps chiefly, from the particular opinions he teaches, and that a Protestant order of the same kind would be almost equally mischievous. Where, then, on my principle, does the evil lie? Here: I believe God has established a universal Church in the world, which grew out of a family and embodies the idea of family life in its highest expansion. That idea stays fresh and real only so long as a strict, unbroken connexion is kept between its highest form and its lowest, so long as calling Him who was, who is, and who is to come "Father" is felt to be no figurative abuse but the only possible explanation of the word's most ordinary use. And I believe Ignatius Loyola founded a universal order on a principle altogether different from this divine one, indeed subversive of it. Such an order cannot preserve any part of the true constitution of society; it must keep interfering with it and substituting something else; and family order and this pseudo-ecclesiastical order must be perpetual, irreconcilable enemies.

4\. If so, I need not argue at length that the spiritual and universal society whose signs we have found should be the great instrument for building up and sanctifying each nation's domestic society, which divided sects, the civil power, and a merely seeming universal fellowship cannot do. It takes the family by baptism into God's family, supposes all civil duties and relations to grow out of these first ones, and connects the highest ecclesiastical duties with ordinary social duties.

II\. 1\. How far religious bodies or sects can be trusted with a nation's *scientific* education can be judged from how they have felt about science at different stages of their history. Almost without exception, a sect at the height of its faith and sympathy has looked on science as wholly alien to faith and irreconcilable with it. The cause may have been a Manichæan horror of the outward world, or dread of it as too holy to touch, or dislike of the slow, cold methods by which its secrets are learned, or, as with the Quakers, a dim intuition of a link between the laws of physical and spiritual investigation that ordinary philosophers had overlooked. The result was the same. A generation or two later, outwardly at least, everything changes. Persons with a genius for physical speculation arise in these sects and devote themselves to it; members generally feel that such pursuits can no longer be discouraged as they were in their fathers' days. A few sturdy Protestants remain to warn the young against perils that a sure instinct tells them are real, while others, who think themselves wiser and more enlightened yet very religious, explain what lessons about the Divine wisdom and goodness may be drawn from natural discoveries. Some find this last change promising; to me the old state was far better. The old teachers acted on a principle: man's business is to know his Creator and do His will, they saw no connexion between these studies and that end, and so they rejected them. Their descendants, when they first take up these pursuits, complain not that the application of the maxim was narrow but that the maxim itself was, since a man who attends properly to his religious duties may give a fair share of time to pursuits with a different aim. Soon the scientific pursuits feel genuine and the religious duties artificial and traditional; if the former are not wholly followed and the latter neglected, there is at least no sympathy between them. Then if a religious revival comes, and the feelings embodied in the sects shape public opinion and create what is called a religious world, science is made to tell a tale about the truth of Scripture and the being and attributes of God. A moral is wrung from its facts by fair means or foul, often by dishonest construction of evidence or suppression of what has been proved. If a fact contradicts a current theological notion or interpretation of Scripture, it is not looked at fairly; the question is raised whether there ought to be such a fact, and so whether we may recognise it if it is one. Scientific men are rightly disgusted. They see that the cause of honesty as well as of science is at stake, and they begin to suspect any hypothesis the more for its gratifying religious feelings.

2\. Here the civil power steps in and says, 'We cannot permit this; our subjects must be taught science fairly and truly. We must have railways and steam-engines. In the present state of society even our handicraftsmen must understand something of the machinery they work, and they need knowledge of many subjects unknown to their ancestors. Your religious men may make what corrections or draw what inferences they please, but we must teach the things; we must give our countrymen a scientific culture.'

I have only this to say in answer: I believe this determination will defeat itself. Teaching a multitude of things is not scientific culture but fatal to it. Those who defend this education like to name Bacon. Would that they studied him with real pains! They would find him denouncing, as one of the main hindrances to scientific knowledge and progress, the desire for facts that are "fructiferous" and not "luciferous," that lead to mere results and not to the search for higher principles. His whole aim was to teach how to seek laws in facts, not how to make from a heap of observations first a theory and then a machine. He blamed the passion for mere effects and so-called practical results for most of the delusions and crimes of the alchymists. If he reappeared today and heard himself praised as the man who taught that making shoes is nobler than seeking principles, he would believe the very mischiefs he delivered his countrymen from were returning through the abuse of his own wisdom. Yet this is the doctrine a merely political statesman inevitably adopts, and it has ever been, and must be, the maxim of State education.

3\. The Jesuits cannot be accused of neglecting to give their scholars information on physical subjects. Nor does it appear that they tried to restore old theories or teach opinions other than those generally sanctioned by the philosophers of their day. As the Dominicans and Franciscans reversed the papal decree against Aristotle, so the Jesuits seem practically to have reversed the decree against Galileo, eagerly using the direction men's minds were taking toward physical inquiries to turn them away from inquiries more immediately concerning themselves. Here, as everywhere, their instruction followed one principle in one coherent system: teach everything, whether physics, history or philosophy, so that the student feels he is not apprehending a truth but receiving a maxim on trust or studying a set of probabilities. On this rule they could publish an edition of the "Principia," noting that its main doctrine had been denounced by the Pope and was therefore to be rejected, while recommending the book for its ingenious arguments and apparent demonstrations. Strange as it seems, there was no curl of the lip in this, nor any dishonesty in the usual sense. The editors did not believe Newton *had* proved his point; they lacked the feeling of certainty needed to think anything could be proved. All is one sea of doubts, perplexities and possibilities, and the great necessity is to feel that we cannot reach truth and must therefore submit to an infallible authority. That was the habit of their minds. The religious man can judge whether it was true when he considers its effect in producing the scepticism of the eighteenth century; the scientific man, when he thinks how hopeless of progress those who hold it must be.

4\. A national Church believes it exists to cultivate the inward man, as the civil power exists for the outward man, and that it has a commission for this. It must therefore witness continually against all these notions of education. It cannot tolerate for an instant the sectarian notion that studying the laws by which God framed this universe is not a solemn, religious work, to be done reverently alongside the study of the laws on which He built the moral universe. As she believes there is a method for knowing the one constitution, so she believes there is a method for knowing the other. The two methods may be connected but cannot be the same. The spiritual method is not honoured when you force physical facts into obedience to it; you are certain they cannot contradict it, and sure they will illustrate it ten thousandfold more than all your moralities laid over them. A national Church must believe, in the highest sense, that what *is* is right. This is the pillar of her own existence, and she sets it against the world's maxim that things are right which we make so by our rules and conventions. So she must teach her children to ask boldly, "What is?", encourage them to expect an answer, and teach them in what frame of mind to wait for it, receive it, and give thanks for it.

This lesson is very unlike the one the civil power tries to teach in its education. The spiritual teacher in his own sphere leads men into the secret heart of things, teaching them the laws of their own being and their direct relation to the Creator. In physical science he must act on the same principle. He cannot merely teach facts and opinions; he must guide his pupil to a knowledge of laws. He will follow this method with the higher or professional classes under his discipline. He cannot change it, though he may entirely alter his scheme of instruction with the lowest classes. They too are men, who want to know and have a right to know the order in which they are placed and the meaning of what they do, and who must not merely be taught what to do or be given rules for doing it.

So it is almost needless to say that such a teacher regards authority not as the Jesuit does, as a substitute for truth, but as what puts us on the right way of searching for it. A national church believes she is set in the midst of a nation by Him who "for this end was born, and for this end came into the world, that He might bear witness to the truth." She is there to bear witness of it, to rebuke the slavish and godless tempers that hinder men from seeking truth in any direction, and by leading them to know it to make them free.

III\. How literature and art are likely to be treated by these different classes can be guessed from the foregoing. Sects in their infancy reject both as worldly and heathenish; in their manhood and decline they tolerate them as necessary indulgences, or try to make them religious by sugaring them over with Christian phrases. The civil power encourages both, because they supply diversion and entertainment to different classes, but judges their value by how far they serve immediate utility. The Jesuit favours the literary diligence of laborious compilations, annals and chronologies, and the art that helps tie devotion more closely to the senses. So in each of these forms of education, from different causes, there is the same tendency to give human utterances, whether in books, pictures, sculpture, music or architecture, an artificial, outward, fictitious character. Either they become insincere expressions of what is actually in men's hearts, or the hearts themselves are made insincere by constantly aiming at some effect to which the names "moral, useful, religious" are, by great abuse of language, applied. But a body that really believes it has a commission to cultivate the mind and spirit of a nation, to call forth what is truest and noblest, awaken reason, understanding and affections, give them their key-note and bring out their harmonies, will feel that the men God has given power to express their own minds and the mind of their age, in words or in sensible forms, have a high vocation and mighty responsibility. It will feel that the influences of the world are likely to choke their powers and keep them from expanding freely and happily, and that the spiritual mother must brood over them with tender care. She will cheer them amid outward and inward discouragement, give them the soothing food and medicine of peaceful devotions and outward images of serenity, stir them with heroic examples, and make them conscious of their relation to past and future. She will hold up high and distant ends, so that they are not crushed by their age or tempted to court its approval. She will humble them that they may be exalted, and teach them to discover the invisible in the visible instead of confounding them and bringing the higher under the conditions of the lower. In training the more illustrious citizens of the commonwealth she is in fact marking out the course by which all should be trained who are to be citizens indeed, for God has committed some trust to each, to be fulfilled for His glory and the good of the land.

IV\. 1\. I will close this subject with a few words on ethics. Strictly speaking, the sectarian does not recognise such a study. He thinks the religious man has been taken into a position altogether different from other men's, and that he and they are not under the same law. A set of rules and maxims must be observed by those who would be members of the family and citizens of the community, and the religious man submits to these, but he is also subject to Gospel influences that the ordinary man has nothing to do with. Christian ethics mean the religion of the heart according to the Bible and apply only to the converted; worldly ethics mean regulating conduct by the maxims received among worldly men and apply to the unconverted. On this showing, a morality for man as man does not exist.

2\. So the statesman interferes, as in the other cases, and says, 'Then you shall teach the morality that belongs to your position, and I will teach that which belongs to mine. Men must acknowledge some rule of life. These subjects of mine, converted or unconverted, must be trained to some sense of their relations to each other. Legal penalties alone are not enough; they must be taught some reason for their conduct, some method of self-government.' Of course these reasons and methods must turn on maxims of self-interest. How could they turn on any other? The statesman has been warned off religious ground, and this is all that remains to him.

3\. I need not speak at length of Jesuit ethics, which are in bad odour among us and probably in most Continental nations. I wish only to remark that all the evil in them flows from the first principle of founding a universal order on a human calculation of what is expedient for preserving the Church and religion. Once you build a society as powerful and coherent as the Jesuit society, the preservation of the order inevitably becomes the one great end to which all others are subordinate. To keep this great machine moving and effective, everything must be sacrificed. I do not think there is one pernicious maxim in the Constitutions that cannot be legitimately deduced from this primary assumption. The Jesuits feel about morality, as about science, not that it *is* but that it *has been made*, and so may be remade for a higher object. The world has framed its maxims to keep itself alive; the Jesuit may frame his to keep the holy religious order alive. The object is surely better, but in both cases the ways are determined by arrangement and convention.

4\. Once again, a national Church exists to protest against these outrages upon the very ground of a nation's existence. It affirms that morality is universal: in its highest form it is meant and attainable for all men, since the covenant of Baptism takes all who will receive it into the highest state a man on earth can enjoy, with all help for resisting the powers of the flesh, the world and the devil that seek to rob him of his human privilege. It affirms that morality is in direct opposition to selfishness, in its lowest forms as well as its highest, since the lawgiver's penalties and the Church's prayers are both aimed at this sin, one denouncing its outward effects and the other seeking to root out the inward disease. It declares that morality, in its lowest forms as well as its highest, is grounded on the character and will of God, subjection to that will being the lesson of the law and conformity to that character the effect of the power of the Gospel. So it must of necessity hate and curse such schemes of morality as the Jesuits have sanctioned. These schemes pervert a truth, that each case has peculiar points and delicate complications requiring wisdom, refinement and freedom from rash judgment in the one who deals with it, into the confounding doctrine that there is no common law of right and wrong, or that the creatures to whom it is addressed have no conscience to perceive it.

I have two remarks before I conclude this head. First, I believe all the defects of national Churches, and of the education they have given, can be traced to a notion far too common among their members and ministers: either that their position is sectarian, that they are merely civil bodies built for civil ends, or that they are merely parts of a religious society or order existing for purposes wholly foreign to those of the civil power. If an opponent produces facts showing the weakness and inefficiency of these Churches, or any one of them, I will gladly consider them. I am satisfied they will all confirm my view, prove the inherent viciousness of the schemes of education that have at various times seemed most plausible, and give another reason why every national Church should understand its high position and zealously assert it. My other remark is addressed to the statesman. He has felt in most European countries, and still feels, the peril of Jesuit influence and the need to guard against it. But what can he do? If he tries to get rid of the idea that there is one Catholic Church in the world, and treats religion as merely private sectarian opinion, he will not stop the Jesuits from entering his dominions and becoming masters of his schools. His tolerant maxims will ease their settlement, and the earnest cry that will rise in a land left to sectarian influence for some united body, some organic fellowship, will make their arrival welcome. Will he then, in despair, resort to his own peculiar powers, proscribe and banish the intruders, or put them to death? He knows these methods have been tried and tried in vain: the crushed order has risen again, its other influences strengthened by the credit of persecution and martyrdom. This subtle, Protean society knows one barrier, and only one, that it cannot break through: a national Church, strong in the conviction of its own distinct powers, paying respectful homage to those of the State, educating all classes to be citizens by making them men. The Jesuit regards that spectacle with wonder and despair. Where such a Church exists he may safely be allowed to walk up and down in the land; the sting of his order is taken away, and he may become a worthy and respectable member of the commonwealth. If the statesman is convinced that maiming and finally suppressing such a Church is the true object of his policy, he must invent some new charm for laying this enemy. None has yet been discovered.

#### [Section VI.—The Modern Interpreters of Prophecy.]{.smallcaps}

There is one class of people I sincerely respect who may be offended by these observations: the modern interpreters of Prophecy. First, they will think that I professed great reverence for the Old Testament as the national history of the Jews, yet overlooked one of its most remarkable features, its promise of permanence and restoration to God's ancient people. In doing so, they will say, I showed indifference to inspired words and a preference for my own theories. Second, they will say that my notion of a Divine constitution already established, one that is national as well as spiritual and universal, in practice sets aside the doctrine of the second coming of Him who will make all things new. Third, they will say that when I spoke of the Romish system as distinct from the Latin Church, I overlooked the Divine word's clear declarations about judgments on the apostasy and the final cutting off of every body that belongs to it.

1\. I agree with those who look for a national restoration of the Jews that much of the language commonly used against their views comes from prejudice and misunderstanding. I do not understand, for instance, what is meant by "*carnal*" when it is used against the doctrine that there are other privileges besides those of the Church as a spiritual body, which the Jews once had and will have again. If I am right, these privileges are as necessary a witness against carnality as those that belong to us as children of God and heirs of the kingdom of heaven. By carnality I mean the inclination of the flesh, which is not subject to the law of God, neither indeed can be. In one sense these privileges are a stronger witness, because they touch more directly the acts of our earthly life, which tend to call carnality forth. And far from this witness belonging less to our age than to earlier ones, I believe our characteristic weakness is to put religion in a middle region. We will not understand it either in its most transcendent character or in its bearing on common, daily doings. So I suspect these phrases about carnality. They show a tendency that the sight of a national commonwealth like the Jewish one would counteract more effectively than anything seen now, or perhaps ever seen in the Catholic Church. Nor do I see how the reappearance of such a commonwealth on its original soil could be anything but a marvellous and glorious testimony to the great scheme that God, to whom all his works are known from the beginning of the world, has been carrying on, and which no human self-will can frustrate. Everything seems to me to point this way. A people as strange as the Israelites could not have been allowed to exist for so many generations without country or polity unless this destiny were in reserve for them. It is a strange and painful effort even to imagine national distinctness lost in men who, in glory and in depression, have witnessed to it for nearly three thousand years. That would be a far more startling miracle than a Hebrew commonwealth in Palestine; but the first miracle would violate all the analogy of God's dealings, while the other would be their natural consummation.

These conclusions seem to me so reasonable that I ask myself why I found them so hard to reach, and why so many people, less hindered than I may be by prejudice or want of faith, still find the same difficulty. I cannot help feeling that the usual way of stating the Jews' claims has been a great obstacle. At one time the modern interpreters seem to expect the Jewish nation to take the place of the universal Church. At another, they seem to expect Jerusalem to be the centre of that Church in the next age, as Rome has tried to be in this one. At another, they seem to believe that in the restoration of all things the Jews will be the one specimen of a true and godly nation. Those who use language that seems to imply these views may not really hold them. But even coming near such notions may rightly alarm good men. If we are to relapse into a national dispensation, and the idea of the universal Church is to be absorbed into that of an exclusive society, then all the promises made to the Fathers are set at naught. The truth with which Old Testament history was pregnant has come to nothing, St. Paul's mighty conflicts to prove it had been brought into the light were in vain, and the last eighteen centuries have been a blank in human history. If the principle is admitted that anywhere, in profane land or holy land, the spiritual Church can find a visible capital for herself, the principle of Romanism is confirmed, and all the sad experiments that showed it to be ungodly are made worthless. If the Jewish nation or Church is to exalt itself in solitary greatness over the ruins of a fallen universe, then Isaiah and the prophets were wrong and the Pharisees of our Lord's day were strictly right. I do not mean only that Isaiah and the prophets looked forward to a universal dispensation, a Church developing out of one nation. I mean that they always speak of Judea, even in their own time, as the centre of a group of countries, each of which was (or was trying to be) a nation. The burden of the "Valley of Vision" is joined to the burdens of Egypt, Moab, Tyre and Damascus. The Jewish nation interprets to each of these what it ought to be. Each is seen as standing in some relation to the Lord God of the Hebrews, as part of His scheme of judgment and mercy, and as threatened by the same Babylonian power. The Pharisees had wholly lost this feeling. They wanted only that Judea should be supreme as Rome was supreme, that it should in fact be the Babylonian monarchy it had testified against for so many generations.
